89.02359pt\raise-21.67221pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bar{F}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 107.447pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 120.45482pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

where
\bar{F}
is exact and countable colimit preserving. Since by definition every nuclear
C^{\ast}
-algebra lies in the kernel of
F
we can conclude
that
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\subseteq{\mathtt{ker}}(\bar{F})
. As
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)\not\in{\mathtt{ker}}(\bar{F})
we conclude that
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)\not\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
.
∎

In view of
3.88
also the following more modest version of
3.86
.
2
is very interesting.

Problem 3.91
.

Do we have
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
.

Let us focus on the
E
-theoretic part of
3.88
. If the answer turns out to be yes, then the answer to
3.91
also has to be yes, simply because every algebra in the bootstrap class is also trace-class exhaustible (see
3.79
.
3
). On the other hand, if one can verify
3.91
, then one reduces
3.88
to showing that every nuclear
C^{\ast}
-algebra satisfies the Künneth formula.

As a final topic in this section, we consider the
\mathbf{\mathbf{Pr}}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{st}}
-dual of
E
given by

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{L}(\mathrm{E},\mathbf{Sp})\simeq{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{\omega}}(\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}},\mathbf{Sp})\simeq{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,K_{G},\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{cfil}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{sep}}},\mathbf{Sp}),

where we have combined
\mathrm{E}\simeq{\mathtt{Ind}}_{\aleph_{1}}(\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}})
and the universal property of
\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}

3.16
with
3.37
.
2
. The functors on the right hand side have precisely the abstract properties of
K
-theory which motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.92
.

The objects of
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,K_{G},\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{cfil}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{sep}}},\mathbf{Sp})
are called generalized
K
-theory functors.

One can can ask whether
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)
is determined by the values
L(A)
for all
generalized
K
-theory functors
L
. Let us emphasize that this question is non-trivial since a generalized
K
-theory functor must preserve filtered colimits
and has codomain
\mathbf{Sp}
. This excludes the functor
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}
which otherwise would lead to a tautology.

Proposition 3.93
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a map in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{sep}}}
.

1.

The map
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(f)
is an equivalence if and only if
L(f)
is an equivalence for all generalized
K
-theory functors
L
.

2.

The map
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(f)
is a strong phantom if and only if
\pi_{{0}}L(f)
is the zero map for all generalized
K
-theory functors
L
.

Proof.

We start by reducing Assertion
1
to Assertion
2
. In order to prove that
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(f)
is an equivalence it suffices to check that its fibre
F
is
0
. Since
\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is compactly assembled
F
is
0
if and only if it is a strong phantom object. Finally, we note that Assertion
2
implies that
F
is a strong phantom object.

It remains to check Assertion
2
. But the latter is a specialization of
2.51
.
∎

Remark 3.94
.

We are not aware of a proof of
3.93
.
1
that does not rely on invoking that
\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is compactly assembled. Accordinly we do not know whether the collection of countable colimit preserving functors
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathbf{Sp}
jointly detects equivalences.

As a corollary of dualizability of
\mathrm{E}
one can even recover the latter from the
\infty
-category of generalized
K
-theory functors. To this end we let

{\mathtt{ev}}_{(-)}:\mathrm{E}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}^{L}({\mathbf{Fun}}^{L}(\mathrm{E},\mathbf{Sp}){,\mathbf{Sp}}),\quad{\mathtt{ev}}_{A}(L){:=}L(A)

be the canonical functor.

Corollary 3.95
.

The canonical functor

\mathrm{E}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{ev}}_{(-)}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{L}({\mathbf{Fun}}^{L}(\mathrm{E},\mathbf{Sp}){,\mathbf{Sp}})\simeq{\mathbf{Fun}}^{L}({\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,K_{G},\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{cfil}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{sep}}},\mathbf{Sp}),\mathbf{Sp})

is an equivalence.

4
Analysis on homotopy categories

4.1
The light condensed topology

In this section we introduce the light condensed topology on the
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-sets of an
\infty
-category which admits finite products and countable filtered colimits. For any object
C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
the functor
\mathbf{Fin}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
sending a finite set
X
to
C^{X}:=\prod_{X}C

left Kan-extends to light profinite sets, i.e., profinite topological spaces which are
{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}
-indexed limits of finite sets. One could try to consider the power
C^{X}
as the object of continuous functions from
X
to
C
. The light condensed topology is a way to make this idea precise.

In the following sets will be considered as discrete topological spaces.
The following notion was recently proposed by Clausen-Scholze as a basis for the development of condensed mathematics.

Definition 4.1
.

A light profinite topological space is a topological space which can be presented as an
{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}
-indexed limit of finite sets.

Remark 4.2
.

If
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
is a system of finite sets indexed by a cofiltered countable category
I
, then

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
is a light profinite space since we can choose a final functor
{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}\to I

and restrict the limit to
{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}
without changeing the resulting space. Furthermore, for
C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
we have an equivalence

C^{X}\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}C^{X_{i}}\ .

∎

We let
\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}}
denote the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Top}}
of light profinite spaces.

Example 4.3
.

The one-point compactification
{\rm Nature}_{+}
of the integers is an object of
\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}}
.
It can be presented as a limit of the system of finite sets

\dots\to\{0,1,2,\infty\}\to\{0,1,\infty\}\to\{0,\infty\}\to\{\infty\}\ ,

(4.1)

where the map
\{0,\dots,n,\infty\}\to\{0,\dots,n-1,\infty\}
sends
i
to
i
for
i\leq n-1
, and
n
and
\infty
to
\infty
.

The light profinite space
{\rm Nature}_{+}
corepresents converging sequences. In other words,
for any topological space
X
, a converging sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
with limit
x
in
X
is the same as a continuous map

{\rm Nature}_{+}\to X
sending
n
to
x_{n}
and
\infty
to
x
.

∎

Construction 4.4
.

Let
\mathbf{Fin}
denote the category of finite sets.
For any
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
with finite products we have a power functor

\mathbf{Fin}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ ,\quad(X,C)\mapsto C^{X}

which is characterised by a natural equivalence

{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C^{X})\simeq\prod_{X}{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C)

for any
B
in
{\mathbf{D}}
.
If
{\mathbf{D}}
is admits countable colimits, then we can
left Kan-extend the power functor in the first variable:

\textstyle{\mathbf{Fin}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(X,C)\mapsto C^{X}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}}
\textstyle{(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(X,C)\mapsto C^{X}}

(4.2)

∎

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be an
\infty
-category with finite products and countable filtered colimits. For objects
B,C
of
{\mathbf{D}}
we
write

[B,C]:=\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C)

for the set of equivalence classes of maps from
B
to
C
in the homotopy category of
{\mathbf{D}}
. If
{\mathbf{D}}
is pointed or stable, then
[B,C]
is naturally a pointed set or abelian group, respectively.
Using (
4.2
) we get a functor

(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}\ni X\mapsto[B,C^{X}]\in{\mathbf{Set}}\ .

The collection of inclusions
(i_{x}:*\to X)_{x\in X}
of the points of
X
induces
the evaluation

{\mathtt{ev}}:[B,C^{X}]\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}(X,[B,C])\ ,\quad\tilde{f}\mapsto(X\ni x\mapsto i_{x}^{*}\circ\tilde{f}\in[B,C])\ .

(4.3)

Definition 4.5
.

The image of the map
{\mathtt{ev}}:[B,C^{X}]\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}(X,[B,C])
is called the set of representable maps.

Definition 4.6
.

The light condensed topology on
[B,C]
is defined as the maximal topology such that all representable maps
X\to[B,C]
from light profinite spaces
X
are continuous.

We will write
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
for the set
[B,C]
equipped with the light condensed topology.

Remark 4.7
.

The following is just a reformulation of the definition:
For any topological space
Y
a map
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to Y
is continuous if and only if the composition
X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to Y
is continuous for all light profinite spaces
X
and representable maps
X\to[B,C]
.

∎

Remark 4.8
.

In general it is not clear that every continuous map
X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
from a light profinite space
X

is representable. But if
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable, and if
B
admits a weakly compact approximation (see
2.21
), then by
4.32
this is true up to a map with values in strong phantoms.
Indeed, by
4.26
the map
X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is also continuous, and
4.32
provides
the corresponding preimage under the evaluation (
4.3
) in

[B,C^{X}]
.

∎

Remark 4.9
.

In order to see that a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
converges to
g
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}

it suffices to provide a map
\tilde{g}
in
[B,C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}]
with evaluations
g_{n}
at the points
n
and

g
at
\infty
. We shall see in
4.27
that if
B
admits a weakly compact approximation, then the converse is also true, i.,e., if the sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
converges to
g
, then there exists such a map
\tilde{g}
.

∎

Lemma 4.10
.

For all
A,B,C
in
{\mathbf{D}}

the composition

[{B},{C}]^{\mathrm{cd}}\times[{A},{B}]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}

is separately continuous.

Proof.

We show continuity in the left argument.
We fix an element
f
in
[A,B]
and must show that
-\circ f:[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is continuous.
To this end we must show that the map

X\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{ev}}(\tilde{g})}[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\xrightarrow{-\circ f}[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is continuous for every light profinite spaces
X
and
\tilde{g}
in
[B,C^{X}]
. But this composition is equal to
{\mathtt{ev}}(\tilde{g}\circ f)
and therefore continuous by definition.

We now show continuity in the right argument.
We fix an element
g
in
[B,C]
and must show that
g\circ-:[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is continuous.
To this end we must show that the map

{X}\xrightarrow{{{\mathtt{ev}}(\tilde{f})}}[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\xrightarrow{g\circ-}[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is continuous for every light profinite spaces
X
and

\tilde{f}
in
[A,B^{X}]
. But this composition is equal to
{\mathtt{ev}}(g^{X}\circ\tilde{f})
and therefore continuous by definition.
∎

Let
\phi:C\to D
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
and
A,B
objects.

Lemma 4.11
.

The map
-\times\phi:[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[A\times C,B\times D]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is continuous.

Proof.

Let
X
be a light profinite space and
f:X\to[A,B]
be a continuous map represented by

\tilde{f}
in
[A,B^{X}]
. Then
the image of
\tilde{f}\times{\mathtt{const}}(\phi)
in

[A\times C,B^{X}\times D^{X}]\cong[A\times C,(B\times D)^{X}]

represents the continuous map
f\times\phi
.
∎

Let
f:A\to B
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
,
C
be an object of
{\mathbf{D}}
, and let
X
be a light profinite space.

Lemma 4.12
.

We assume that
f
is weakly compact. If
X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is representable, then the composition
X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}

factorizes over a finite quotient of
X
.

Proof.

Let
X\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}X_{i}
be a presentation of
X
as a limit of finite sets with surjective structure maps.
Then
C^{X}\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in{\rm Nature}}C^{X_{i}}
. We consider the square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.0434pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.0434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.45613pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-25.57895pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.0434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C^{X_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.35962pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-7.0434pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.62732pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.62732pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C^{X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

where
i
in
{\rm Nature}
and the dotted completion exists by the weak compactness of
f
.
This means that
X\to[A,C]
factorizes over the finite quotient
X\to X_{i}
.
∎

For the following we assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is pointed and recall
2.38
of a strong phantom map.

Lemma 4.13
.

The
kernel of
{\mathtt{ev}}:[B,C^{X}]\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}(X,[B,C])
consists of strong phantom maps.

Proof.

Let
g
be in
[B,C^{X}]
and assume that
{\mathtt{ev}}(g)=0
. Then

i^{*}_{x}(g)=0
for all
X
in
x
.
Let
f:A\to B
be a weakly compact morphism.
We must show that
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C
vanishes.

Let
X\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}X_{i}
be a presentation of
X

as a countable limit of finite sets. We can assume without loss of generality that the structure maps
X\to X_{i}
are surjective for all
i
in
{\rm Nature}
.
We have an equivalence
C^{X}\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in{\rm Nature}}C^{X_{i}}
. Since
f
is weakly compact
we have a factorization

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C^{X_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C^{X}}

for some
i
in
I
. Let
i_{x_{i}}:*\to X_{i}
be the inclusion of some point.
By assumption there exists a preimage
x
of
x_{i}
under
X\to X_{i}
. We then have

0=i_{x}^{*}(g\circ f)=i_{x_{i}}^{*}h
. Since
(i_{x_{i}}^{*})_{x_{i}\in X_{i}}:[A,C^{X_{i}}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\prod_{x_{i}\in X_{i}}[A,C]
is an isomorphism
we can thus conclude that
h=0
.
This implies
g\circ f=0
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

One could ask under which conditions the map
[B,C^{X}]\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}(X,[B,C])
in (
4.3
) is injective.
This is clearly the case when
X
is finite. But already in the case of
X={\rm Nature}_{+}
we get the map
[B,C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}]\to\prod_{x\in{\rm Nature}_{+}}[B,C]
. For giving a counter example we assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable and admits countable products.
Then
\bigoplus_{x\in X}{C}
is a summand in
C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}
.
If
{B}
is not a compact object in
{\mathbf{D}}
, then we can not exclude the possibility of having a non-zero map
{B}\to\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C
whose components are all zero. Consider e.g. the boundary map

\Omega(\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C/\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C)\to\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C
of the fibre sequence
extending the canonical map
\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C\to\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C
.
∎

For the rest of this section we assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable. Then we have abelian groups
[A,B]
and
4.10
has the following consequence.

Corollary 4.15
.

The addition
[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\times[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is separately continuous.

Proof.

The sum is given by the composition

\displaystyle[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\times[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(-\oplus{\mathtt{id}}_{B})\times{\mathtt{id}}_{[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}}}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle[A\oplus B,B\oplus B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\times[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{id}}_{[A\oplus B,B\oplus B]^{\mathrm{cd}}}\times({\mathtt{id}}_{A}\oplus-)}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle[A\oplus B,B\oplus B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\times[A\oplus A,A\oplus B]^{\mathrm{cd}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle[A\oplus A,B\oplus B]^{\mathrm{cd}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{fold}\circ-\circ{\mathtt{diag}}}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\ .

We conclude
by a multiple application of
4.11
and
4.10
.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

In view of

4.10
and
4.15

one could ask whether the composition or addition is continuous.
The difficulty consists of
understanding continuous maps out of the product. One can check that the composition

X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\times[A,B]^{\mathrm{cd}}\xrightarrow{\circ}[A,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is continuous if the first map has representable
components. But this does not suffice to conclude continuity of
\circ
.
As a consequence we do not know whether
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is a topological group in general.

∎

Recall the notion of a summable sequence from
2.42
.
1
.
We assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable.

Lemma 4.17
.

If
(g_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a summable sequence in
[B,C]
, then
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}g_{n}=0
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
.

Proof.

We have a fibre sequence

\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}C\xrightarrow{i}C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}\xrightarrow{i_{\infty}^{*}}C\ .

(4.5)

Let
\hat{g}:B\to\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}C
witness the summability of the sequence.
We then get a map
\tilde{g}:=i\circ\hat{g}:B\to C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}
which vanishes at
\infty
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

We will see in
4.31
that the converse is also true provided
B
admits a weakly compact approximation.
∎

4.2
The shape topology

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
an
\infty
-category with countable filtered colimits.
In this section we introduce
the shape topology on the
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-sets
[B,C]
of
{\mathbf{D}}
as the minimal topology such that
the maps
[B,C]\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
become continuous for all weakly compact maps
A\to B
.
This topology is very well-behaved and coincides with the light condensed topology provided
{\mathbf{D}}
has finite products and
B
has a weakly compact approximation, see
2.21
.
Similarly, many further assertions shown in this section depend on the assumption that certain objects admit weakly compact approximations. But
note that if
{\mathbf{D}}
is compactly assembled, then every object of
{\mathbf{D}}
has a weakly compact approximation.

For objects
A,C
of
{\mathbf{D}}
we write
[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
in order to stress that we consider this set with the discrete topology.

Let
B,C
be two objects of
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Definition 4.19
.

The shape topology on the set
[B,C]
is the minimal topology such that
the map
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
is continuous for every weakly compact map
A\to B
.

Remark 4.20
.

The following is just a reformulation of the definition:
For any topological space
X
a map
X\to[C,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is continuous if the composition
X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
is continuous for every weakly compact map
A\to B
.

∎

Lemma 4.21
.

Assume that
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a weakly compact approximation of
B
. Then the shape topology
on
[B,C]
is the minimal topology such that the maps
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[B_{n},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
are continuous.

Proof.

Let
[B,C]^{\mathrm{ap}}
denote the set
[B,C]
equipped with the minimal topology such that the maps
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[B_{n},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
are continuous for all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
.
It is clear that the identity map
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{ap}}
is continuous.

Assume now that
f:A\to B
is a weakly compact map. Then there exists a factorization

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}

for some
n
in
{\rm Nature}
. We get a factorization

[B,C]^{\mathrm{ap}}\to[B_{n},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}\xrightarrow{(\tilde{f})^{\ast}}[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}

of
f^{*}
as a composition of two continuous maps.
This shows that the identity map
[B,C]^{\mathrm{ap}}\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is also continuous.
∎

Corollary 4.22
.

If
B
admits a weakly compact approximation, then for every
C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
the topological space
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
has countable neighbourhood bases.

Proposition 4.23
.

If
B
admits a weakly compact approximation,
then the composition

[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\times[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}

is continuous.

Proof.

In view of
4.20
, for every weakly compact map

A^{\prime}\to A
we must show that the restriction

[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\times[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A^{\prime},C]

is locally constant.
Let
(f,g)
be a point in
[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\times[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
. Then there exists a neighbourhood
U_{f}
of
f

on which
[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A^{\prime},B]
is constant with value
f_{|A^{\prime}}
. Since
B
admits a weakly compact approximation
we can choose a factorization

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}

for some weakly compact map
B^{\prime}\to B
. There exists a neighbourhood
V_{g}
of
g

on which
[B,C]\to[B^{\prime},C]
is constant with value
g_{|B^{\prime}}
.
It follows that the composition map is constant on the neighbourhood
U_{f}\times V_{g}
of the point
(f,g)
.
∎

Let
\phi:C\to D
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
and
A,B
objects.

Lemma 4.24
.

The map
-\times\phi:[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A\times C,B\times D]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is continuous.

Proof.

Let
(s,t):E\to A\times C
be a weakly compact morphism. Then
s
is weakly compact and we have a commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.97467pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.83578pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.52882pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-\times\phi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.97467pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.97467pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern D]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.96452pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-22.97467pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[E,B]^{\mathrm{disc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.67842pt\raise-26.44293pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.79709pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(-,t^{*}\phi)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.60031pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.60031pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[E,B\times D]^{\mathrm{disc}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

We must show that the right-down composition is continuous, but this clear since the down-right composition is continuous by definition of the shape topology on
[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.
∎

In order to consider in addition the light condensed topology on the
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-sets of
{\mathbf{D}}
we now assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
has finite products in addition to countable filtered colimits.

Proposition 4.25
.

If
B
has a weakly compact approximation, then for every
C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
the light condensed and shape topologies on the mapping groups
[B,C]
coincide.

Proof.

The following lemma shows that the condensed topology is always finer than the shape topology.

Lemma 4.26
.

For all
B,C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
such that
B
has a weakly compact approximation
the identity map of underlying sets
is a continuous map

[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}

Proof.

We use
4.20
. We must show that for every light profinite space
X
and continuous map

X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
which is represented by an element of
[B,C^{X}]
, and any weakly compact map
A\to B
, the composition

X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[A,C]

is locally constant. But this follows from
4.12
.∎

In order to show that
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
is also continuous we will show that
converging sequences in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
also converge in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
. Since by
4.22
the shape topology has countable neighbourhood bases this implies continuity.

Let
(f_{i})_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
be a sequence which converges to
f
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.

Lemma 4.27
.

If
B
has a weakly compact approximation, then
there exists
\tilde{f}
in
[B,C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}]
with values
f_{i}
at all integers
i
and the evaluation

f
at
\infty
.

Proof.

We use the presentation of
{\rm Nature}_{+}

explained in
4.3
. We choose a weakly compact approximation
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
of
B
.
We can then choose a strictly increasing sequence
(i(n))_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
of integers such that

f_{i(n)|B_{n}}\simeq f_{i(n)+1|B_{n}}\simeq f_{i(n)+2|B_{n}}\simeq\dots\simeq f_{|B_{n}}

for every
n
in
{\rm Nature}
. For every
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we define the morpism

\hat{f}_{n}:=((f_{0})_{|B_{n}},\dots,(f_{i(n)})_{|B_{n}},(f_{|B_{n}})):B_{n}\to C^{\{0,\dots,i(n),\infty\}}

Next we provide commutative squares

\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{f}_{n}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C^{\{0,\dots,i(n),\infty\}}}
\scriptstyle{s^{\ast}}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{f}_{n+1}}
\textstyle{C^{\{0,\dots,i(n+1),\infty\}},}

where
s:\{0,\dots,i(n),\infty\}\to\{0,\dots,i(n+1),\infty\}
is the structure map. This is equivalent to providing for each
k
in
\{0,\dots,i(n+1),\infty\}
a commutative diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.82759pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.76506pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.50938pt\raise-10.01556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.13611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{s(k)|B_{n}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.08896pt\raise-30.17117pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.06654pt\raise 7.36111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.86111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hat{f}_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.82759pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-26.1478pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.82759pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern C^{\{0,\dots,i(n),\infty\}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.0202pt\raise-16.74055pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.21251pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{pr}}_{s(k)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.0202pt\raise-26.1478pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.82759pt\raise-33.48111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.85248pt\raise-26.15887pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.53886pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{k|B_{n+1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.08896pt\raise-33.48111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.08896pt\raise-33.48111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

For the right upper triangle, we choose the canonical filler. For the left lower triangle, we can also choose the canonical filler if
s(k)=k
(i.e., if
k
is in
\{0,\dots,i(n)-1,\infty\}
). If
k
is in
\{i(n),\dots,i(n+1)\},
then
s(k)=\infty
and we choose an arbitrary filler which exists by the stabilization assumption. We thus get a map of systems

\hat{f}:(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\to(C^{\{0,\dots,i(n),\infty\}})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
.

We claim that the map

\tilde{f}:B\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B_{n}\xrightarrow{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\rm Nature}}\hat{f}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C^{\{0,\dots,i(n),\infty\}}\simeq C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}

has the desired properties.
For
k
in
{\rm Nature}
the composition
i_{k}^{*}\circ\tilde{f}:B\to C^{{\rm Nature}_{+}}\to C

is given by the colimit of a system
(f_{k|B_{n}})_{\{n\in{\rm Nature}\mid k\leq i(n)\}}
which coincides with the constant system

(f_{k|B_{n}})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
on the partially ordered subset
\{n\in{\rm Nature}\mid k\leq i(n-1)\}
. We conclude that
i_{k}^{*}\circ\tilde{f}\simeq f_{k}
for all
k
in
{\rm Nature}
.
Furthermore,
i_{\infty}^{*}\circ\tilde{f}
is the colimit of the constant system
(f_{|B_{n}})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
and therefore

i_{\infty}^{*}\circ\tilde{f}\simeq f
.
∎

Using
4.9
and
4.27
we see that if a sequence in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
converges, then it converges in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
. This finishes the proof of
4.25
. ∎

We consider
B,C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
and
let
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a weakly compact approximation of
B
.

Lemma 4.28
.

The canonical map
p:[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}[B_{n},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
presents the target as the Hausdorff quotient of the domain.

Proof.

The map
p
is surjective and open by definition of the shape topology.
Let
p_{n}:[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[B_{n},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}
denote the canonical map.
Let
f
be a point in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
. Then by
4.21
we have an equality
{p^{-1}(p(f))=}\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}p^{-1}_{n}(\{p_{n}(f)\})=\overline{\{f\}}
. Any continuous map
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to X
to a Hausdorff space
therefore uniquely factorizes set-theoretically through the quotient
p
and it is straightforward to see that this factorization is again continuous since
p
is open.
∎

Form now on we assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable and countably cocomplete.

Corollary 4.29
.

[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is a topological abelian group for all
B,C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Proof.

Let
A\to B
be a weakly compact morphism.
Then we have a commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 47.26671pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-42.03403pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\times[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.88908pt\raise 5.58334pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{+}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.88306pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.88306pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 93.90007pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-47.26671pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}\times[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.19725pt\raise-27.40668pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{+}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.26671pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.26671pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[A,C]^{\mathrm{disc}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

We must show that the right-down composition is continuous, but this is clear since
the down-right composition is continuous by definition of the shape topology on
[B,C]
.
∎

We consider
B,C
in
{\mathbf{D}}
and
let
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a weakly compact approximation of
B
. Then we
have a Milnor exact sequence

0\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}}^{1}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}[B_{n},\Omega C]\to[B,C]\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}[B_{n},C]\to 0\ .

(4.6)

Proposition 4.30
.

The closure of
0
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is the subgroup of strong phantom maps and coincides with the image of the first map in the Milnor sequence.

Proof.

It is clear from
2.41
that the kernel of the second map of the Milnor sequence consists precisely of the strong phantoms. In view of the definition of the shape topology and
4.21
this kernel is the intersection of all closed neighbourhoods of
0

and thus coincides with the closure of
0
.
∎

Corollary 4.31
.

If
B
has a weakly compact approximation, then a sequence in
[B,C]
is summable if and only if it converges to
0
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.

Proof.

By
4.30
almost all members of a zero sequence in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}

are strong phantoms. By
2.46
such a sequence is summable.
Conversely, by
4.17
a summable sequence in
[B,C]
converges to zero
in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{cd}}
, and therefore also in
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.
∎

The following is a generalization of
4.27
from
{\rm Nature}_{+}
to general light profinite spaces
X
.
But note that here we assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable and the conclusion is weaker.

Proposition 4.32
.

If
B
has a weakly compact approximation, then for
any continuous map
f:X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
there exists a map
\phi:X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
with values in strong phantom maps
such that
f-\phi
is representable.

Proof.

We choose a weakly compact approximation
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
of
B
.
Let
f:X\to[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
be a continuous map.
By
4.20
the restrictions
f_{|B_{n}}:X\to[B_{n},B]
are locally constant.
Since
X
is a light profinite space every locally constant map out of
X
factorizes over a finite quotient. Let
(X\to X_{i})_{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}
be the cofiltered system of finite quotients of
X
.
Then
C^{X}\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}C^{X_{i}}
.
The map
f
gives thus rise to an element in
(f_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}
in
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}[B_{n},C^{X_{i}}]
, where
f_{n}
is represented by the map
B_{n}\to C^{X_{i(n)}}
representing
f_{|B_{n}}
,
and where
X\to X_{i(n)}
is precisely the quotient over which the latter factorizes.
By the surjectivity of the second map in the Milnor sequence (
4.6
) the image of
(f_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}
under
the canonical map

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}[B_{n},C^{X_{i}}]\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}[B_{n},C^{X}]

lifts to an element
\hat{f}
in
[B,C^{X}]
. We let
f^{\prime}:={\mathtt{ev}}(\hat{f}):X\to[A,B]^{cd}
. Then by
4.30
the difference
\phi:=f-f^{\prime}
takes values in strong phantom maps.
∎

4.3
Hausdorff quotients

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be an
\infty
-category with countable filtered colimits.
In this section we analyse the Hausdorff quotients of the spaces
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.

Let
B,C
be objects of
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Definition 4.33
.

We let
\overline{[B,C]}
denote the Hausdorff quotient of
[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.

Lemma 4.34
.

If
A,B
admit weakly compact approximations, then
the composition map has a continuous factorization

\textstyle{[B,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\times[A,B]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[A,C]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{[B,C]}\times\overline{[A,B]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{[A,C]}}

over the Hausdorff quotients.

Proof.

The upper horizontal map is continuous by
4.23
.
This implies that the composition map in the lower sequence is continuous, too, since the maps to the Hausdorff quotients are open as seen in the proof of
4.28
.
∎

We consider a system
(B_{n,m})_{(n,m)\in{\rm Nature}\times{\rm Nature}}
. We assume that
(B_{n,m})_{m\in{\rm Nature}}

is a weakly compact approximation of
B_{n}:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{m\in{\rm Nature}}B_{n,m}
.
Finally we set
B:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B_{n}
and
let
C
be in
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Lemma 4.35
.

We have an isomorphism of topological spaces

\overline{[B,C]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\overline{[B_{n},C]}\ .

Proof.

The diagonal
{\rm Nature}\to{\rm Nature}\times{\rm Nature}
is cofinal and

(B_{n,n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a weakly compact approximation of
B
.
We get isomorphisms

\displaystyle\overline{[B,C]}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}[B_{n,n},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{(n,m)\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}[B_{n,m},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{m\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}[B_{n,m},C]^{\mathrm{disc}}\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}\overline{[B_{n},C]}

using
4.28
in the first and last step.∎

The main point which makes
4.35
work is that the system
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}

has a functorial choice of systems of compact approximations. It is not clear that
such a choice exists in general. But if the objects
B_{n}

admit shapes for all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
(see
2.7
), then we can rigidify the choice of approximations by assuming that they represent the shapes.

Proposition 4.36
.

For any system
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
{\mathbf{D}}
with
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B_{n}\simeq B
such that
B_{n}
belongs to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{shp}}
for all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we have an equivalence

\overline{[B,C]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}\overline{[B_{n},C]}\ .

Proof.

We get a system
(S(B_{n}))_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{\aleph_{1}}({\mathbf{D}})
. For every
n
we choose a system
(B_{n,m}^{\prime})_{m\in{\rm Nature}}
in
{\mathbf{D}}
such that
S(B_{n})\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{m\in{\rm Nature}}y(B_{n,m}^{\prime})
.
We now define a system
(B_{n,m})_{(n,m)\in{\rm Nature}\times{\rm Nature}}

inductively by
n
such that
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{m\in{\rm Nature}}y(B_{n,m})\simeq S(B_{n})
. Then by
2.23
the system
(B_{n,m})_{m\in{\rm Nature}}
is a weakly compact approximation of
B_{n}
for every
n
in
{\rm Nature}
and we can deduce the assertion from
4.35
.

Assume we have constructed the system
(B_{n,m})_{\{0,\dots,k-1\}\times{\rm Nature}}
.
Then we consider the map
S(B_{k-1})\to S(B_{k})
in
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{\aleph_{1}}({\mathbf{D}})
, using the usual
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}-\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}
-formula for the mapping anima in the
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-category, as a point in the anima

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{m\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathrm{op}}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{m^{\prime}\in{\rm Nature}}{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(B_{k-1,m},B^{\prime}_{k,m^{\prime}})\ .

This point can be represented by a diagram

\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k-1,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k-1,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k-1,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k,m^{\prime}(m-1)}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k,m^{\prime}(m)}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k,m^{\prime}(m+1)}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}

for some increasing function
m^{\prime}:{\rm Nature}\to{\rm Nature}
. We then set
B_{k,m}:=B_{k,m^{\prime}(m)}^{\prime}
.
This finishes the induction step.
∎

From now one we assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable.
Recall the
2.21
of a weakly compact exhaustion. Let
X

be a light profinite space.

Proposition 4.37
.

We assume that
B
admits a weakly compact approximation.

1.

We have a canonical surjective map of sets

\overline{[B,C^{X}]}\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\overline{[B,C]})\ .

2.

If
B
admits a weakly compact exhaustion, then this map is a bijection.

Proof.

The map is given by the lower horizontal map in

\textstyle{[B,C^{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{ev}}}
\textstyle{{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,[B,C])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{[B,C^{X}]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\overline{[B,C]})}

which exists since if
f
in
[B,C^{X}]
is strong phantom, then
i_{x}^{*}f
in
[B,C]
is strong phantom for every
x
in
X
. Here we use that by
4.30
the kernels of the left- and right vertical maps are given by
phantom maps or maps which have values in phantom maps, respectively.

We now show that this map is surjective.
We fix a weakly compact approximation
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
for
B
. We consider
f
in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\overline{[B,C]})
.
Let
\hat{f}:X\to[B,C]
be any set-theoretic lift. Then
X\xrightarrow{\hat{f}}[B,C]\to[B_{n},C]

coincides with
X\xrightarrow{f}\overline{[B,C]}\to[B_{n},C]
which is continuous.
Since
n
is arbitrary we conclude by
4.21
that
\hat{f}
is continuous.
By
4.32
there exists
\tilde{f}
in
[B,C^{X}]

which represents
\hat{f}
up to a map with values in strong phantom maps.
This implies that the image of
\hat{f}
in
\overline{[B,C]}
is the desired preimage of
f
.

We now assume that
(B_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a weakly compact exhaustion for
B
and show injectivity.
Let
\bar{f}
be in
\overline{[B,C^{X}]}
and assume that it goes to zero in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\overline{[B,C]})
.
Let
\tilde{f}
in
[B,C^{X}]
be a preimage of
\bar{f}
.
In order to check that
\bar{f}=0
it suffices to check that the image of
\tilde{f}
under
[B,C^{X}]\to[B_{n},C^{X}]
vanishes for all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
.

Let us fix
n
in
{\rm Nature}
. Then the image of
\tilde{f}
in
[B_{n+1},C^{X}]
is the image of a map
\tilde{f}^{\prime}
in
[B_{{n+1}},C^{Y}]
under the canonical map

[B_{{n+1}},C^{Y}]\to[B_{{n+1}},C^{X}]
, where
X\to Y
is a quotient to a suitable finite set
Y
.

Since
i_{x}^{*}\tilde{f}
is a strong phantom map for every
x
in
X
we know that

i_{x}^{*}\tilde{f}=i_{y}^{*}\tilde{f}^{\prime}
is a strong phantom map, where
y
is the image of
x
in
Y
.
Since the structure map
B_{n}\to B_{n+1}
is weakly compact
we can now conclude that
the image of
\tilde{f}^{\prime}
under
[B_{n+1},C^{Y}]\to[B_{n},C^{Y}]
vanishes.
This implies that the image of
\tilde{f}
under
[B,C^{X}]\to[B_{n},C^{X}]
vanishes.
∎

Assume that all objects of
{\mathbf{D}}
have weakly compact approximations.

Definition 4.38
.

We can form the additive topologically enriched category
\bar{\mathbf{D}}
with the same objects as
{\mathbf{D}}
, and with the topological morphism groups

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\bar{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C):=\overline{[B,C]}

between objects
B,C
.

The composition in
{\mathbf{D}}
is well-defined and continuous
by
4.34
.
We have a functor

\bar{\mathrm{ho}}:{\mathbf{D}}\to\mathrm{ho}{\mathbf{D}}\to\bar{\mathbf{D}}\ .

Recall from
Section

2.2
that
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{shp}}
is the full subcategory of objects
C
admitting shapes. By
2.9
it is stable and closed under countable colimits.
Then
4.36
says:

Corollary 4.39
.

The restriction
\bar{\mathrm{ho}}_{|{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{shp}}}:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{shp}}\to\bar{\mathbf{D}}
preserves countable filtered colimits.

By
4.37

we have:

Corollary 4.40
.

For any two objects
B,C
of
{\mathbf{D}}

we have a canonical surjective map

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\bar{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C^{X})\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\bar{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C))

which is bijective if
B
has a weakly compact exhaustion.

Proposition 4.41
.

If
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable and compactly assembled,
then the functor
\bar{\mathrm{ho}}:{\mathbf{D}}\to\bar{\mathbf{D}}
is conservative.

Proof.

Let
f:B\to C
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
such that its image

\bar{f}
in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\bar{\mathbf{D}}}(B,C)
is invertible.
Let
\bar{g}
in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\bar{\mathbf{D}}}(C,B)
be an inverse of
\bar{f}
represented by a morphism
g:C\to B
. Then
\phi:={\mathtt{id}}_{B}-g\circ f
is a phantom.
We can conclude by
2.49

that
g\circ f={\mathtt{id}}_{B}-\phi
is an equivalence.
Similarly we show that
f\circ g
is an equivalence.
This implies that
f
is an equivalence.

∎

4.4
Analysis in
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

We have two interesting examples of compactly assembled
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
(see
3.68
) and
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
(see
Theorem

3.67
) to which we can specialize the
general results about the topological
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-spaces and groups from the preceding sections. We note that both categories are pointed, left-exact and admit countable filtered colimits which commute with finite limits.
Furthermore,
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is stable.
By
3.50
the homotopy category of
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
can be identified with the homotopy category of asymptotic morphisms
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
from
[
GHT00
]
, and (see e.g.
3.29
) the homotopy category of
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is the classical equivariant
E
-theory category
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
.

In the non-equivariant case the
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-sets of
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
have been equipped with topologies in
[
CS24
]
. In this section we show that these topologies coincide with the ones induced by
the compactly assembled
\infty
-category
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, and that many of the results from

[
CS24
]
are special cases of the statements shown in the preceding sections.
Similarly, in the case of
E
-theory we will show that the topology on the
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-groups of
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
constructed in the non-equivariant case in
[
CS24
]
coincides with the one
induced by the compactly assembled
\infty
-category
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, and that the statements about this topology
from
[
CS24
]
are again special cases of the general facts shown in in the preceding sections.

A new aspect is that we can compare the analysis in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{sep}}}
with the analysis in
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
or
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

via the functors
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
(see
3.16
) or
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
(see (
3.40
)).

For
B
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
we have two functors

(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

X\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))\ ,\quad X\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)^{X}\ ,

where the second uses the power functor (
4.2
).
Similarly we have functors
(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

X\mapsto a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))\ ,\quad X\mapsto a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)^{X}\ .

Proposition 4.42
.

We have natural equivalences of functors

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)^{-}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(-,B)):(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

(4.7)

\displaystyle a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)^{-}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(-,B)):(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ .

Proof.

We consider the case of
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. The case of
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is analoguous.
We first construct the natural transformation in (
4.7
).
Since the domain
is defined by a left Kan extension
along
\mathbf{Fin}^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathbf{ProFin}^{\aleph_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}
, it suffices to construct a natural transformation of the restriction

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)^{-}\to\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(-,B)):\mathbf{Fin}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ .

(4.8)

Since both functors in (
4.8
) send finite disjoint unions to products (for the right-hand sides this follows from the exactness of
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, and in the case of
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
we employ Schochet exactness)
they are both left Kan-extensions of their restriction to
*
.
The canonical equivalence

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(*,B))\simeq\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)^{*}

thus fixes an equivalence (
4.8
).
If
X\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
is a presentation of
X
as a countable cofiltered limit of finite sets, then

C(X,B)\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}C(X_{i},B)
. Since

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
preserves countable filtered colimits by
3.20
(or
3.46
for
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
),
we see that (
4.7
) is an equivalence, too.
∎

Proposition 4.43
.

For every separable compact Hausdorff space
X
and
A,B
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
we
have canonical maps

\displaystyle[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]^{\mathrm{sh}})

\displaystyle{}[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]^{\mathrm{sh}})\ .

If
X
is a light profinite space, then their images consist precisely of the
representable maps.

Proof.

We again consider the case of
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. The case of
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is analoguous.
For the first assertion we must show that the map of sets

[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}(X,[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)])

sending
f
in
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]
to the function

x\mapsto i_{x}^{*}f
takes values in continuous maps. Continuity of a map out of a separable compact Hausdorff space
can be tested with sequences.

Let
f
be an element in
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]
.
We consider a continuous map

t:{\rm Nature}_{+}\to X
.
Then we have a square in
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 67.33728pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-53.12195pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 105.55261pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 105.55261pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}(X,[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.29115pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-67.33728pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C({\rm Nature}_{+},B))]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.33728pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 91.33728pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Set}}}({\rm Nature}_{+},[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}])}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

It suffices to show that the right-down composition sends
f
to a continuous map. But this is the case in view of
4.42
since this map is representable by
t^{*}f
in
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C({\rm Nature}_{+},B))]
.

The second assertion is a consequence of
4.42
.
∎

Recall that from
3.6
that
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is topologically enriched.
The following consequence of
4.43
is a version of
[
CS24
, Prop. 2.4]
.

Corollary 4.44
.

For all
A,B
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
we have canonical continuous
maps

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}_{GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}(A,B)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]^{\mathrm{sh}}\ .

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}_{GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}(A,B)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]^{\mathrm{sh}}

By this corollary the functors
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
and
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
refine to functors between topologically enriched categories.

The following result is only for
E
-theory since we employ
4.37
in the proof which has only been shown under a stability assumption.

Corollary 4.45
.

For every light profinite space
X
we have a surjective map

\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C(X,B))]}\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]})\ .

Proof.

This follows immediately from
4.42
and
4.37
.
∎

Example 4.46
.

If
u
is in
U(M(B))^{G}
(
u
is a
G
-invariant unitary multiplier on
B
) and
f:A\to B
is a homomorphism, then

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(f)\simeq\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(u^{*}fu)
.
Assume that
(u_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a sequence in
U(M(B))^{G}
such that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}u_{n}^{*}fu_{n}=g
in
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}_{GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}(A,B)
. Then
f
and
g
are called approximately
unitary equivalent.

Corollary 4.47
.

If
f
and
g
are approximately unitary equivalent, then

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(f)-\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(g)
is a strong phantom morphism.

Proof.

We note that
(\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(u_{n}fu_{n}^{*}))_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is the constant sequence and that by
4.44
we have an equality

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(u_{n}fu_{n}^{*})=\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(g)
.
This implies the result in view of
4.30
.
∎

The following is a consequence of
4.41
and
4.47
.

Corollary 4.48
.

If
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to A
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
are such that

f\circ g
and
g\circ f
are approximately unitary equivalent to isomorphisms, then

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(f)
is an equivalence.

As a consequence of
2.47
we get:

Corollary 4.49
.

If
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to C
are homomorphisms
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
which are approximately unitarily equivalent to zero, then
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(g\circ f)=0
.

It is clear that
g\circ f
is again approximately unitarily equivalent to zero so that
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(g\circ f)
is a phantom map by
4.47
. But the fact that this class vanishes is less trivial.

∎

Example 4.50
.

An exact sequence

0\to A\to B\to C\to 0

in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
will be called weakly quasi-diagonal
[
MT00
]
if there exists a family of invariant projections
(p_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}

in
M(B)
satisfying:

1.

For all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we have
p_{n}B\subseteq A
.

2.

For every
b
in
B
we have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}(p_{n}b-bp_{n})=0
.

3.

For every
a
in
A
we have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}p_{n}a=a
.

In
[
MT00
]
it was in addition required that the family
(p_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is increasing.
If
p_{n}
in
A
for all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
, then the exact sequence is called quasi-diagonal.

Proposition 4.51
.

The boundary map
\delta:\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)\to\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)
associated to a weakly quasi-diagonal extension is a strong phantom map

Proof.

We consider
p:=(p_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
as a multiplier of
\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B
.
We let
\tilde{B}
be the subalgebra of
\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B
generated by

\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B
,
{\mathtt{diag}}(B)
and the elements
p{\mathtt{diag}}(b)
for all
b
in
B
.
The projection
q:\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B\to\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C
sends
the generators of
\tilde{B}
to elements of the subalgebra
\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C+{\mathtt{diag}}(C)
.
We therefore get a map of exact sequences

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 260.61401pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 260.61401pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 125.83237pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 125.83237pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\tilde{B}/\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 222.75153pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 222.75153pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 260.61401pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 260.61401pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

where
\tilde{A}:={\mathtt{ker}}(q)\cap\tilde{B}
and the two left vertical maps are induced by the diagonal.
The multiplier
p
induces an invariant central projection in
M(\tilde{B}/\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}B)

which splits the lower sequence. Consequently, its boundary map
\bar{\delta}:\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)\to\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}A)
vanishes. Now
\bar{\delta}
is equivalent to the composition

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)\xrightarrow{\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathtt{diag}})}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A})\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}A)

Let
g:\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(D)\to\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)
be a compact morphism in
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
Since

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(D)\xrightarrow{g}\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)\xrightarrow{\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathtt{diag}})}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A})\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}A)

vanishes, by compactness of
g
and since
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is exact and preserves sums,
there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(D)\to\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)\xrightarrow{\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathtt{diag}})}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A})\to\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}A)

vanishes. Recall that
\tilde{A}
is a subalgebra of
\prod_{n\in{\rm Nature}}A\cong{\mathtt{ker}}(q)
so that we can consider the projection

{\mathtt{pr}}_{n+1}:\tilde{A}\to A
onto the
(n+1)
th component. It induces a projection
\bar{\mathtt{pr}}_{n+1}:\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}A\to A
.
The composition

A\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{diag}}}\tilde{A}\to\tilde{A}/\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}A\xrightarrow{\bar{\mathtt{pr}}_{n+1}}A

is the identity of
A
. This implies that

\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(D)\xrightarrow{g}\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)
vanishes.
Since
g
was an arbitrary compact morphism to
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)
we conclude that
\delta
is a phantom map.
∎

In the case of a trivial group
G

in
[
Sal74
]
,
[
Sch05
]
,
[
MT00
]
the set of classes in

\mathrm{KK}_{1}(A,B):=[\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C),\Sigma\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)]^{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}}

classifying quasi-diagonal extensions has been identified with the closure of zero in a natural topology on the
\mathrm{KK}
-groups.
If
C
is in the bootstrap class, then the comparison map
c
from (
3.73
) induces an isomorphism

[\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C),\Sigma\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)]^{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\to[\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C),\Sigma\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)]^{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}}

identifying these closures and
4.51
recovers these classical results in view of

4.30
.
Note that the papers listed above also study the converse question: Does the fact that the extension class is a strong phantom imply that the extension is quasi-diagonal?

∎

Remark 4.52
.

In this remark we explain how parts of the results of

[
CS24
]
are direct consequences of the fact that
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
or
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
are the homotopy categories of the compactly assembled
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
or
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and the general statements from the preceding sections.
Everywhere we use that these categories are known to be compactly assembled by
Theorem

1.5
which implies the existence of compact approximations.

1.

The shape topology on
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
or
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
coincides with the topology on the
E
-theory groups or
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-sets in
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}

introduced

[
CS24
]
. This follows from the characterization of the latter topology
in
[
CS24
, Prop. 2.6]
together with
[
CS24
, Prop. 2.8]
which is the same as the characterization of the shape topology
in
4.21
. Here we use the fact that a shape system for
B
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}

provides a weakly compact approximation
of
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)
in
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, or
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)
in
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
(assuming that
B
is twice suspended and
K_{G}
-stable), see
3.69
.

2.

The topologies on
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]
or
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]
introduced in
[
CS24
]
a priori depend on the choice of the separable
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
.
It only follows a posteriori from the continuity of the composition

[
CS24
, Thm. 2.15]
that they only depend on the classes of
A
and
B
in
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
or
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
.
In contrast, the shape topology is by definition an invariant of the classes of
A
and
B
in
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
or
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.

3.

The topological spaces
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
or
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
are first countable by
4.22
. This corresponds to
[
CS24
, Thm. 2.10]
.

4.

A converging sequence in
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
or
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
is
by definition a continuous map
{\rm Nature}_{+}\to[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C))]^{\mathrm{sh}}
or
{\rm Nature}_{+}\to[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
, respectively. By
4.27
and
4.42
such a sequence can be represented by elements of
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C({\rm Nature}_{+},C))]
or
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C({\rm Nature}_{+},C))]
, respectively. The converse is clear: any element in
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C({\rm Nature}_{+},C))]
or
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C({\rm Nature}_{+},C))]
gives rise to a converging sequence by
4.42
,
4.9
and the equality of the shape and light condensed topologies
4.25
.
There facts are
[
CS24
, Thm. A]
and
[
CS24
, Thm 2.12]
, where they are
called Pimsner’s Condition.

5.

By
4.23
the composition in
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}
or
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}

is jointly continuous for the shape topology. This corresponds to
[
CS24
, Thm 4.5]
and
[
CS24
, Thm 2.15]
.

6.

4.30
and
4.36
describes the Hausdorffifications of the groups
[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
and the spaces
[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}
.
In this way we reproduce
[
CS24
, Thm. 3.10]
and the corresponding parts of
[
CS24
, Thm. 4.6]
.

7.

By
4.20
for any compact map
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)\to a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)
the composition

\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}_{GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(B,C)\to[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}\to[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{sh}}

factorizes over a discrete space. By
3.70
this is in particular the case if the map
a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A)\to a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)
comes from a semi-projective map
A\to B
. This corresponds to
[
CS24
, Prop. 2.8]
.

8.

By
4.34

the composition maps

\overline{[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}\times\overline{[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]}\to\overline{[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}

and

\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}\times\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)]}\to\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}

are continuous. This corresponds to

[
CS24
, Prop. 3.2]
and the part of
[
CS24
, Thm. 4.6]
.

9.

By
4.28
combined with
3.70
we have isomorphisms of topological groups or spaces

\displaystyle\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A_{n}),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{disc}}

\displaystyle\overline{[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A_{n}),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]^{\mathrm{disc}}\ ,

where
(A_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a shape system for
B
and we assume that
B

is twice suspended and
K_{G}
-stable in the case of
\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
This corresponds to

[
CS24
, Thm. 3.7]
except for the countability assertions
which is specific to the
C^{*}
-algebra situation and not a consequence of the general
\infty
-categorical considerations.

10.

We can form a topologically enriched additive category
\overline{\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
(denoted by
EL
in
[
CS24
]
) and the topologically enriched category
\overline{\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}}
with the same objects as
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}

and with topological morphism groups

\overline{[\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}
or spaces
\overline{[a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B),a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C)]}
.
The composition is well-defined by
4.52
.
8
.
By
4.39
the functors

\overline{\mathrm{e}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\overline{\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\ ,\qquad\overline{a^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\overline{\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}}

preserves filtered colimits.
These are
[
CS24
, Thm. B]
and
[
CS24
, Thm. 3.10]
.

11.

By
4.41
the functor
\mathrm{E}^{G}\to\overline{\mathrm{E}^{G}}
is conservative.
This
recovers
[
CS24
, Prop. 4.7]
.
Again there is no analogue for
\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}\to\overline{\mathrm{A}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep},0}}
since
4.41
requires stability.
